0x014a7f67	a	?UserMusicArbiter
0x014a7f7f	a	?The UserMusicArbiter plug-in allows gamers take control of the playback,  turning off the title music track to play their own. The tile will regain control of the playback immediately after users stop playing the custom music track. 
0x014a806b	a	 To use, simply drop an instance of this into any plug-in chain that you would like disabled once user music starts.  There is no attribute to set, nor is there any event to trigger in this plug-in. 
0x014a8137	a	?TimeStretch
0x014a8160	a	rw::audio::core::TimeStretch - Samples Buffers
0x014a8190	a	The TimeStretch plugin provides the capability for users to stretch or shorten a sound while preserving its pitch and spectral characteristics. This is in contrast to time stretching by means of resampling, which changes the pitch of the sound in order to change its length. On the other hand, the TimeStretch plugin can also be used in conjunction with a Resample plugin to achieve a time-invariant pitch shift. For example, a sound could be pitched up a third octave (~= 26%) with the length preserved by stretching it by a factor of 1.26 using the time stretcher combined with pitching it up with the Resample plugin by the same factor of 1.26.
0x014a8419	a	This plugin is of type PLUGINTYPE_INPUT_PROCESS. It can only reside in a source voice, which is a special voice that contains an input source at the top (PLUGINTYPE_INPUT_SOURCE), and possibly various other source voice plugins such as a resample or rechannel. The reason it can only reside in a source voice is because it relies on being able to produce a different number of output samples from the number of input samples it receives. Non-source voice plugins must produce the same number of samples as they receive.
0x014a8622	a	The TimeStretch plugin uses a time domain based approach to altering the length of a sound. This is in contrast to frequency domain based approaches that must first convert the signal to the frequency domain (typically via the FFT). Both approaches have advantages and disadvantages associated with them, and there is no single ideal time stretching method that works well on all types of audio content in all processing situations. This particular time stretcher is designed to work well in the stretching and contraction of speech content, typically with stretch ratios in the range of 0.75 to 1.5. In contrast to a frequency domain based approach, it achieves this with less or no chorusing and phasing artifacts, no transient smearing, and a much lighter processing load.
0x014a892b	a	The basic mechanism used in achieving a stretch or contraction involves analyzing the sound waveform and determining successive locations at which a high degree of correlation exists. With the knowledge of these high correlation points, entire sections between these points can be looped to achieve a stretch, or cut to achieve a contraction. The correlation analysis operation is by far the most processor-intensive part of the stretching algorithm, and has been optimized to use as few cycles as possible. The user has some control over how this algorithm is used via several construction parameters, so that cycle usage or efficiency can be traded off against functionality and quality. These are discussed next.
0x014a8bf8	a	The TimeStretch plugin supports single and multi-channel operation, even though it is expected that most speech sources being stretched will be single channel. In the case of multi-channel operation, the user can select between MULTICHANNELMODE_INDEPENDENT_CHANNELS and MULTICHANNELMODE_COMBINED_CHANNELS modes at voice contruction. These modes differ only in how the correlation points are found for each of the channels. Since this analysis operation is expensive, it is possible to use a kind of mean best correlation point across all channels and thereby save doing the analysis on each channel. This corresponds to the MULTICHANNELMODE_COMBINED_CHANNELS mode, and results in only a slightly higher processing load than what would be experienced in processing a single channel. This mode works well if the different channels all still have a relatively high degree of correlation with each other, such as would be the case if they were all derived from the same speech source, but perhaps have had different effects applied to them, such as different types or amounts of reverberation, filtering, or background noise. In these cases, this mode might also be preferred over the independent channel mode (described below) due to it keeping the stretching and contracting of all channels synchronized. This synchronization occurs because the cut/loop points are identical for all channels, and so all channels will loop or cut at the same times.
0x014a91a0	a	If, however, there is little or no correlation between some or all of the channels of a given multi-channel source, use of the MULTICHANNELMODE_COMBINED_CHANNELS mode can result in a poor quality stretched or contracted signal. In that case, the MULTICHANNELMODE_INDEPENDENT_CHANNELS mode should be used, so that each channel is analyzed and processed separately. This mode will produce a processing load that is proportional to the number of channels present, so that for example, stretching a stereo source will utilize about the same number of cycles as stretching two separate mono sources. If not explicitly specified, the time stretcher defaults to this mode of operation.
0x014a9448	a	The other contruction parameter that has a great bearing on the amount of processor used is the Efficiency setting. This setting also relates to how the algorithm that finds the peak correlation points operates. Specifically, the algorithm first looks at a coarse granularity for the peak correlation point (within the correlation window), and then refines this peak location and value by moving at single sample granularity towards the true peak by moving in the direction of positive slope. The Efficiency setting, which ranges from a minimum of 1 (EFFICIENCY_1) to a maximum of 10 (EFFICIENCY_10), sets the granularity of the coarse search. The default setting of EFFICIENCY_6 corresponds to a coarse granularity of 16 samples, which seems to provide a good balance between efficiency and audio quality for most signals.
0x014a9781	a	The only parameter under the user's control during operation of the plugin is the stretch ratio (ATTRIBUTE_STRETCHRATIO). This sets the factor or ratio by which the signal is stretched. A value of less than 1 results in contraction of the signal, while a value of greater than 1 results in elongation. The theoretical maximum stretch ratio that can be achieved with this algorithm is 2.0, and the minimum ratio (contraction) is 0.5. As such, this is the range of allowed values that may be specified by the user. However, in practice, the mean maximum range that will be achievable over time for a typical audio signal will be 0.625 to 1.75. Furthermore, in MULTICHANNELMODE_INDEPENDENT_CHANNELS mode, in order to avoid potentially large disparities in the amount of buffer required by one channel versus another, the stretch ratio is internally limited to the minimum range possible for any signal at any time. This corresponds to a range of 0.75 to 1.5.
0x014a9b3e	a	Note also that when the ATTRIBUTE_STRETCHRATIO is set exactly to 1, the plugin switches to a mode where it simply passes through the input data without any time stretch processing. This eliminates the CPU load normally associated with the time stretcher, and allows the game code to save cycles when they are not needed.
0x014a9c88	a	This attribute sets the ratio by which the signal is stretched. A value of less than 1 results in contraction of the signal, while a value of greater than 1 results in elongation. Ratios in the range of 0.5 to 2.0 may be specified. The minimum guaranteed range of stretch ratios is 0.75 to 1.5. In the case of MULTICHANNELMODE_INDEPENDENT_CHANNELS mode, requested ratios outside of this 0.75 to 1.5 range will be internally clamped. In MULTICHANNELMODE_COMBINED_CHANNELS mode, the algorithm will attempt to achieve the requested ratio (between 0.5 and 2.0), but will on average over time not be able to achieve anything beyond the range of 0.625 to 1.75.

0x014a9fd0	a	efficiency
0x014a9fe0	a	Defines whether or not channels are grouped together for the purpose of finding correlation peak locations within the signal. Grouping results in an overall cycle usage comparable to that used in processing a single channel signal, but might result in some audio degradation when applied to signals with uncorrelated channel content. 
0x014aa130	a	MultiChannel Mode
0x014aa144	a	multiChannelMode
0x014aa158	a	milliseconds
0x014aa168	a	Defines the size in milliseconds of the time stretch algorithm window. The window is the fundamental grouping of samples used by the time stretch algorithm to determine correlation peak locations for the purposes of looping and cutting signal segments. The window must be at least large enough to contain one cycle of the lowest expected frequency component in the signal so that a legitimate peak correlation point can be found. The default window size of 9 milliseconds, for example, corresponds to a period of 111 Hz, so that any signals with a fundamental frequency greater or equal to this can be successfully stretched or contracted by the algorithm.

0x014ab2a8	a	The SubMix is a "source" PlugIn, and it must be inserted alone at position 0 on the voice. The SubMix's input channels must be set to 0, and the output channels must set to either 1, 2, 4, or 6 channels.
0x014ab375	a	If a SubMix is destroyed, any PlugIns connected to it will be automatically disconnected in a safe manner.

0x014ab728	a	SndPlayer1 StreamLoopFileName
0x014ab74b	a	JSndPlayer
0x014ab758	a	SndPlayer1 RequestHandle and RequestExternal array
0x014ab790	a	The SndPlayer1 PlugIn is the main player used for game audio sample playback. It efficiently supports resident RAM samples, streamed samples, gigastream samples, stitched playback, looped samples, sample accurate starting, up to 64 channels, and multiple compression types.
0x014ab8a3	a	Looping samples will repeat their loop section infinitely, there is no lead-out section present which can be triggered.
0x014ab91c	a	When stitching, the samples being stitched can be in any format (different sample rates, compression types, number of channels), and the joining of two samples will still be seamless.
0x014ab9d5	a	A SndPlayer1 instance will always output a constant number of channels, but the source sample data may be of any channel configuration. If the source and output channels do not map 1:1, then a standard fold-up or fold-down will occur if the channel configurations are standard (1, 2, 4, or 6 channels). If either the sample source or PlugIn output channel configurations are non-standard, then a simple re-mapping will occur. If there are less source sample channels than output channels, the remaining output channels will be silent. If there are more source sample channels than output channels, the left over source sample channels will be dropped.  To determine the number of channels in an SndPlayer asset at runtime you can use the static member function SndPlayer::GetFileInfo()
0x014abce8	a	Gigastream formatted samples are those where a small portion of the beginning of a sample is always stored in RAM, and the rest is stored on media such as DVD. The idea is to allow a sample to begin with no delay as if it was in RAM, but also allow the sample to be as long as desired without consuming any RAM besides the small beginning part in RAM. The small part of the file stored in RAM gives the SndPlayer1 time to retrieve the next portion off of DVD. This means that the length of sample in RAM must be longer than the length of time it takes to retrieve more data off of DVD, under all game conditions where heavy stream load is occurring. The amount of sample data in RAM is chosen at the time the SndPlayer assets are created. You may need to experiment with the length of the data in RAM, a good starting point is about 1 second of sample data.
0x014ac043	a	SndPlayer1 is one of the few PlugIns that supports non-standard channel configurations (up to 64). The Route PlugIn can be used to bus selections of channels to SubMix PlugIns, which can be useful for efficiently streaming layers of interactive music or ambience. For example, a stream file could be authored that contains 4 intensities of stereo crowd ambience. The 8 channel SndPlayer1 output can be routed into 4 stereo SubMixes, and then each SubMix Voice can vary its volume appropriately to cross-fade between the 4 intensities. This approach may use more CPU time than other approaches, but streaming a single 64 kilobyte/s stream will be far easier on a DVD than streaming 4 16 kilobyte/s streams. In most cases DVD bandwidth will be far more scarce than CPU bandwidth.
0x014ac350	a	The play event allows SndPlayer sample files to be played.
0x014ac38c	a	The Play event is asynchronous, meaning any output attributes the SndPlayer1 supports will not be immediately updated to reflect the new state of the SndPlayer1.
0x014ac42f	a	Note that any sample requests with loop points in them will prevent subsequent requests from playing properly since a looping sample will never end (unless stopped with rw::audio::core::SndPlayer1::EVENT_STOP). Due to other internal implementation details queuing further requests while a looping sample is in the queue is forbidden. An assert will be generated if such an attempt is made. Seeking into giga or looped samples is not supported.

0x014aca55	a	It is possible that under certain circumstances this event will under-report the amount of data, but as long as sensible use is made of isFullyBuffered teams should not run into trouble. Also if the request is a loop with a lead-in section then it is possible that only data for the lead-in will be reported on. 0x014ae3dc	a	startTime
0x014ae3e8	a	Start time
0x014ae3f8	a	This parameter can be used to trigger the starting of sample playback at an exact point in time with sample accuracy. The units are in seconds, and are not relative.
0x014ae49f	a	To trigger starting of a sample in the future, first obtain the current system time from rw::audio::core::System::GetTime(), and then add how many seconds in the future you want the sound to be triggered at to the system time.
0x014ae583	a	If startTime values less than the current system time are encountered, the request will play without any delay as soon as all previous requests finish playing. A value of 0.0 will always play the sample as soon as possible.
0x014ae664	a	The startTime is applied to all requests, even if they are queued up behind each other. When the current request finishes playing, the next request's startTime is evaluated, and the sample will either stitch directly to the end of the previous request if the startTime was less than the current system time at that point, or the sample will wait until the system time reaches it's start time. Note that the startTime will never force a request to prematurely interrupt a request ahead of it in the queue.
0x014ae85e	a	If playback needs to be scheduled in the near future (e.g. the next few hundred milliseconds), then the Play event should be called from an RWAC timer callback. This will ensure that your code is called in a timely manner before the system time has a chance to pass you by.
0x014ae971	a	Note that although the startTime is specified with very high precision that allows sample accurate results, rounding errors may occur in some cases that could cause the sample number to be off by 1 sample from where the desired results are. This would most often be a problem if attempting to start a new sample relative to another sample that is playing and being pitched, as the other sample position will be located at a fractional position within a single sampling point, due to the nature of resampling. If possible, the stitching mechanism provided by queueing samples one after another in a single SndPlayer1 instance should be used if seamless stitching is required.

0x014b3df8	a	The ReverbModel1 plug-in is a parameterized model-based reverberator that allows some user control of the size and characteristics of a reverberant space.  Each instance takes in a single (mono) source, and fans out a reverberated spatialized signal to the selected number of output channels (except the LFE in 6 channel case). The reverberator is comprised of 6 parallel comb filters followed by a series allpass filter. A degree of spatialization is achieved by using slightly different parameters for the allpass on one output channel versus another. Control over the reverb is achieved via 3 parameters: the reverb space size, reverberation time, and brightness. Each of these is associated with an attribute, and described there.
0x014b40d8	a	Since the ReverbModel1 PlugIn will tend to be used as a global effect, it is configured so that it will return nothing when turned off (ATTRIBUTE_SETREVERBTIME = 0). In other words, the voice that the ReverbModel1 PlugIn resides in will not produce audio output to the voice(s) it feeds in this case. Normally, a separate parallel path would exist that persists even when the reverb is off, providing the dry, non-reverberated audio signal. 
0x014b4298	a	This attribute allows for the adjustment of the perceived brightness of the reverb. By increasing the brightness, the reverberated signal will retain proportionally higher frequency components. This is achieved by increasing the cutoff frequency of each lowpass filter in the comb filter feedback loop. By selecting a brightness of 1 (the default), the frequency content of the reverb will be matched with that defined by the model. It is recommended that this default normally be used. However, tweaking of this parameter may be useful to emulate effects such as the humidity of the environment (i.e., higher humidity corresponds to a less bright sound, since the moisture absorbs higher frequencies).

0x014b492b	a	?ReverbIR1
0x014b4950	a	The ReverbIR1 plug-in provides an efficient method for applying the reverberation characteristic of a given space to the game audio signal. This is achieved by performing the fast convolution of the signal with the impulse response (captured or created) of a given reverberant space. The efficient implementation is achieved by performing the convolution in the frequency domain, after having first sectioned the impulse response into smaller pieces in order to minimize delay and memory usage. To apply a given impulse response, a given impulse response file (such as say a stereo .wav file that contains the IR) must first be converted to an irf file (via SIMEX/SX). This file must be at the same sample rate as the output mode used by the game. The response is then applied to the signal via the EVENT_APPLYREVERB event. While the PlugIn has been designed for efficient operation, it does still require considerable resources, especially memory. To help mitigate this, memory usage can be traded off against the high frequency content of the reverberated signal retained. This is specified when creating the impulse response file (irf) via the SIMEX/SX parameter percentHighFrequencyCut. Reductions in memory usage will be nearly proportional to the numberspecified (i.e., if 50.0 is specified, memory usage will go down by close to half). To also minimize cycle usage, the SIMEX/SX parameter samplesPerFFTBlock allows for specification of the FFT block (segment)size. Larger sizes mean more efficient processing, but also add delay. Sizes used by the FFT must be a power of 2, so any sizes specified that aren't will be forced to a power of 2. The ReverbIR1 plug-in supports several channel-impulse response file configurations. 1:1 source to output channel mappings (1, 2, 4, or 6 channels) are supported, where the impulse response file is either mono, or it is comprised of the same number of channels as the signal input. Also, a 1:n source to output channel configuration is supported (1, 2, 4, or 6 output channels), where the impulse response file is comprised of n channels.
0x014b5178	a	Since the ReverbIR1 PlugIn will tend to be used as a global effect, it is configured so that it will return nothing when inactive. In other words, the voice that the ReverbIR1 PlugIn resides in will not produce audio output to the voice(s) it feeds in this case. Normally, a separate parallel path would exist that persists even when the reverb is off, providing the dry, non-reverberated audio signal.
0x014b530c	a	Also note that most impulse response files will have some gain associated with them. If this is significantly larger than one and the plugin input signal is also fairly large, the ReverbIR1 output will most likely get clipped in the mastering voice, causing distortion. This can be avoided by decreasing the send level into or out of the ReverbIR1 voice, or by reducing the level via a gain plugin within the ReverbIR1 voice. 
0x014b54b8	a	This event will stop the reverb that was applied with EVENT_APPLYREVERB, clear all resources used in the application of that particular reverb file, and stop the audio output from the associated voice.

0x014b5957	a	?Resample
0x014b5967	a	?Pitch is specified in linear units. A pitch of 1.0 will pass the signal through without modification, a value of 0.5 will drop the pitch an octave, and a value of 2.0 would increase the pitch by an octave.
0x014b5a37	a	A value of 0.0 will stop playback entirely. Note that in this case the sample at the current playback position will be repeated until the pitch is set to anything but 0.0. This can create a DC offset when the resulting signal is mixed in with other audio, which can in turn cause nasty clipping and other distortion. If the pitch is set to 0.0, then gain for the voice should also be set to 0.0 to avoid this DC offset.
0x014b5bdc	a	Pitch values have a direct linear effect on the rate certain components (for example decoders) have to produce and process sample data. The components and plugins affected are only those upstream of the Resample plugin. The pitch can therefore have a direct effect on the amount of CPU consumed by these plugins. If pitch is set to 4.0, a decoder will use four times as much CPU than if the pitch was set to 1.0. Some components upstream of the resample may only be able to produce a certain amount of samples before an undesired side effect will occur. For example, the XMA decoder will not be able to produce enough samples to keep up with higher pitch values, and stuttering or CPU blocking will occur if stressed. It is generally recommended that pitch values above 2.0 not be used, however there area number of situations where pitch values above 2.0 would be fine. Lower source sample rates for example allow more headroom for pitching a sample up. If pitch values that are too high for the given components they will typically be clamped and printf type warnings to the console may be generated in debug configs.
0x014b603d	a	Unlike other plugins, pitch change can only be affected on source voices with this method. This is because pitch changes affect the rate samples are requested or produced at through the signal chain. In order to keep the general purpose plugin case simple and solve all sorts of other pitch synchronization issues, RenderWare Audio Core only allows pitch changes near the top of a source voice. After that, all sample data is at a uniform sample rate which makes processing it far simpler. This restriction introduces ramifications to the various end users and other technology control layers used to drive RenderWare Audio Core. Instead of being able to simply plunk down one or more pitch controls on source and submix voices, to achieve desired effects such as singular control at a submix point and doppler, control layers must maintain a virtual mapping of all the desired control points, compute a final pitch for each source voice, and apply it.
0x014b63f7	a	 Another side effect of not performing pitch changes on a signal at the actual location desired is that frequency sensitive plugins (for example a low pass filter) will not react the same way as if the signal had actually been pitch changed. For example, a voice might have desired a situation where a low pass filter set the signal to  100 Hertz, then a doppler effect was applied through a pitch control. Imagine the doppler was such that the sample was traveling away from the listener to the point where the pitch would normally be halved. If the pitch change was actually made on the signal there, the frequency spectrum would be shifted from 0..100 Hertz to 0..50 Hertz. As RenderWare Audio Core doesn't allow pitch control at these points, the controlling layers of RenderWare Audio Core would have to compensate instead if a more accurate representation of frequency spectrum manipulation due to Doppler and similar pitch changes is desired. In this case, any frequency sensitive plugin could have its frequency control attribute scaled by the virtual pitch requests that are desired downstream of it. The low pass filter cutoff frequency in our above example would be multiplied by 0.5 and end up at  50 Hertz instead of 100 Hertz.

0x014b791b	a	?PeakingIir2
0x014b7938	a	The PeakingIir2 plug-in is a relatively inexpensive filter that can boost or cut a selected part of the signal spectrum. The filter provides up to +/- 25 dB gain in the selected band and rolls off to 0 dB gain elsewhere. This 0-dB gain characteristic allows for the filters to be chained together in series, such as in a parametric EQ application, since they do not attenuate or magnify the signal outside of their gain region. The filters are capable of a roll-off of at least 12 dB per octave with the appropriate Q selection (Q is a measure of the narrowness of the filter). The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear. 
0x014b7c88	a	This attribute specifies the filter quality or Q factor. A filter's Q is defined as the ratio of its center frequency to it's bandwidth. As such, narrow bandwidths correspond to high Q values and vice versa.
0x014b7d58	a	ATTRIBUTE_SETQ
0x014b7d68	a	This attribute specifies the filter gain at the center frequency. It is defined in linear, not decibel (dB) terms, and expresses the ratio of amplitudes between the filter input and output signals. As such, a gain of 1 does not magnify the signal at all, and corresponds to 0 dB.

0x014b81c7	a	?Pan3D
0x014b81f8	a	rw::audio::core::Pan3D::Delay line, internal buffer and DSPSettingsLocal
0x014b8248	a	The Pan3D plug-in allows for spatializing a mono signal over a 5.1 channel configuration. This plug-in is intended for use with the RWAudio3D library. RWAudio3D is responsible for producing a set of parameters or DSP coefficients which is to be used by Pan3D in processing the audio input signal.
0x014b8372	a	The Pan3D plug-in consists of a multi-tap delay line, a number of filters, and a number of Vector Base Amplitude Panning (VBAP) panners. The length of the delay line is determined by the constructor parameter maxDelaySeconds, and the number of read taps is determined by the number of reflections (a parameter in the DSPSettings struct) plus 1 for the direct path. The positions of the read taps are determined by the delay values of the direct and reflected paths. The input audio signal is first read either directly from a tap, or via interpolation in the case of a changing delay and corresponding Doppler shift. It then passes through a low pass filter, followed by a low shelving filter in the case of the direct path only, and finally into an improved VBAP panner. The low pass filter simulates air and surface absorption effects while the low shelving filter models the proximity effect. The VBAP panner maps the direct path azimuth to gain values for each of the 5 main channels in the 5.1 setup to allow for a smooth panning transition when the sound emitter is very close to the listener. The reflected paths use 2 channel panning on the assumption that reflections are normally far away, thereby reducing the amount of memory needed.
0x014b8851	a	The current implementation of the Pan3D plug-in uses the IIR2 class for both the low pass and the low shelving filter, and uses CopyWithGainRamp() and MixWithGainRamp() to apply the gain matrices. The delay line interpolation is implemented as a two-point linear interpolation. In the case where the sound source is located at such a great distance that the size of the delay line required exceeds maxDelaySeconds and the user still requires the Doppler effect, the Pan3D plug-in will use a time-invariant implementation of a pitch shifter which is based on crossfading two modulated delay lines. 
0x014b8aa8	a	The Pan3D plug-in accepts a mono source as the input signal and produces a 6 channel output. If rendering of stereo or multichannel sources is required, the user will have to first split the input into multiple mono sources.
0x014b8b8a	a	Pan3D can be CPU intensive if a large number of reflections are being processed. The majority of CPU time is spent on the filtering, but the delay line interpolation also counts for a significant amount of time. The plug-in can require a large amount of memory as well, depending on maxDelaySeconds and the number of reflections used, and therefore it is good practice to understand how much of the system resources can be allocated to Pan3D before choosing these values. 
0x014b8d64	a	For more information on 3D audio calculation and rendering, please see the RWAudio3D packagedocumentation and library.
0x014b8de0	a	This event will stop the spatialization and stop the audio output from the associated voice.
0x014b8e40	a	Stop Pan3D
0x014b8e50	a	This event results in the DSPSettings object location being passed to Pan3D. The object is also copied to local memory owned by the Pan3D. The reason for copying the DSPSettings object to Pan3D local memory is to minimize the time duration during which the applicationcreating and filling in the DSPSettings object (such as RWAudio3D) and Pan3D are accessingthe same memory space. Given this approach, the only time during which a conflict could occur is during this event, when the data of DSPSettings is being copied over. As such, it is essential that the RWAC lock (rw::audio::core::System::Lock()) be acquired by the application whenever it is modifying the DSPSettings object. In the case of RWAudio3D, for example, this would be whenever the application calls the Calculate() method of that package to update the DSPSettings object values.

0x014bd1b3	a	?LowShelfIir2
0x014bd1d0	a	The LowShelfIir2 plug-in is a relatively inexpensive filter that can boost or cut the signal spectrum from DC to a selected cutoff frequency.The filter provides up to +/- 25 dB gain in the gain region, and rolls off at approximately 12 dB per octave to a gain of 0 dB elsewhere. This 0-dB gain characteristic allows for the filter to be chained in series with other filters supporting this characteristic, since it does not attenuate or magnify the signal outside of its gain region. As such, the filter can be useful in a parametric EQ application, where it could provide what is traditionally referred to as bass control. The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear. 
0x014bd548	a	This attribute specifies the filter gain. It is defined in linear, not decibel (dB) terms, and expresses the ratio of amplitudes between the filter input and output signals. As such, a gain of 1 does not magnify the signal at all, and corresponds to 0 dB.
0x014bd648	a	This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value of 0 Hertz or less is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum unmodified, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value greater than or equal to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) will be clamped to the maximum allowable frequency (very near the Nyquist frequency) such that the filter remains stable (poles within the unit circle). This results in the specified gain being applied to the entire spectrum.
0x014bd843	a	?LowPassIir2
0x014bd857	a	?The LowPassIir2 plug-in allows for the relatively inexpensive low pass filtering of a signal. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a transition band roll-off of at least 12 dB per octave.The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear; though the degree of non-linearity in the pass band is not severe. Filter stability is assured by clamping the cutoff frequency (internally) to always be BETWEEN zero Hertz and the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate).
0x014bdb10	a	This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value greater than the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value of 0 Hertz or less will be clamped to the minimal allowable frequency (very near 0 Hertz) such that the filter remains stable (poles within the unit circle). This results in the maximum amount of filtering.
0x014bdccf	a	?LowPassFir64
0x014bdce7	a	?The LowPassFir64 plug-in is a high quality linear phase lowpass filter with excellent isolation. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a minimum of 50 dB stop band attenuation with a very narrow transition bandwidth in between.The filter uses only feed-forward elements, and as such can be classified as a Finite Impulse Response (FIR) filter. The filter is of order 64, utilizing 64 delay elements, and possessing the same number of zeros in its transfer function. Given its FIR nature, the phase response of the filter is linear, and the filter is unconditionally stable.
0x014bdf38	a	This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value greater than the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value of 0 Hertz or less will be clamped to 0 Hertz, resulting in the maximum amount of filtering.
0x014be083	a	?LowPassButterworth
0x014be0a0	a	The LowPassButterworth plug-in provides users with a low pass version of the classic analog IIR Butterworth filter. The Butterworth filter frequency response is referred to as being maximally flat in the passband. What this means in practice is that the pass band frequency response of the filter is very flat, not exhibiting the ripples seen in other filter types, such as the Butterworth's analog cousins, the Chebyshev and Elliptic filters. Also, the frequency response is referred to as being monotonic in the pass and stop bands, which means that as frequency increases, the amplitude of the response never increases, but rather will either stay the same or decrease. The filter gain in the passband is normalized to 0 dB. Near the filter cutoff frequency (3 dB point), the rolloff of the filter will be around 6 dB per octave per order or pole (see ATTRIBUTE_SETFREQUENCY description). As such, users can select the filter order based on their needs. For example, if the use case requires a filter with maximum rolloff in order to cut out any frequencies beyond a certain value, the maximum filter order should be chosen. On the other hand if the use case requires a very gentle rolloff in order to model some real world physical acoustic phenomenon (such as for example occlusion), then perhaps selecting a lower order (such as 1) makes the most sense. These filters are guaranteed to be stable, somethingthat is inherent in the digital Butterworth filter design process. 
0x014be668	a	This attribute specifies the filter order, which is equivalent to the number of delay elements in either the feedforward or feedback paths. The number of feedback or feedforward coefficients is equal to the order + 1. A higher order filter will exhibit a steeper rolloff than a lower order one. In the case of purely feedback filters with no feedforward terms, the rolloff is generally around 6 dB per octave (doubling of frequency) per order. However the addition of feedforward terms (which the Butterworth filters have) makes the relationship between order and rolloff less straightforward. These feedforward terms contribute 'zeroes' to the transfer function response, which, for example, makes for a very steep slope as the frequency approaches the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate). Near the cutoff frequency, however, one can expect a slope of roughly 6 dB per order. 
0x014be9e0	a	Order
0x014be9e8	a	ATTRIBUTE_SETORDER
0x014bea00	a	This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. The cutoff frequency is the frequency at which the amplitude response of the filter is 3 dB below its value in the passband (nominally 0 dB). This is also sometimes referred to as the knee of the frequency response. A value greater than the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value of 0 Hertz results in the maximum amount of filtering. Note that in order to avoid excessively long filter coefficient calculation times on some platforms (PC), ATTRIBUTE_SETFREQUENCY is clamped to stay within 1% of the Nyquist frequency at both DC at the low end of the spectrum, and the Nyquist frequency at the high end. Given an output sample rate of 48000 Hz for example, this would result in a minimum cutoff frequency of 240 Hz (1% of 48000/2), and a maximum (before the filter turns off) of 23670 Hz (48000/2 - (1% of 48000/2)). 
0x014beddb	a	?Limiter1
0x014bedef	a	=The Limiter1 plug-in provides a means for limiting the signal level.  When the mean magnitude of the signal exceeds some settable threshold, a gain term is applied to the signal to reduce the overall amplitude. This gain will cut in quickly, over a fixed attack time of 50 milliseconds (TBD if we need to change this). The gain will be fully released when the mean magnitude is below the threshold (including hysteresis) for a user specified release time. The gain amount is based on a fixed ratio of 10:1, whereby a 10 dB change in input signal level will result in a 1 dB change in output signal level. The gain is derived on a per channel basis, or can be based on the maximum mean magnitude channel across all channels. It should be noted that since the limiter threshold must be exceeded by the signal mean magnitude (via a lowpass filter with very low cutoff), in order to ensure that signal peaks that far exceed the mean are also compressed, a threshold that is much lower than those peaks must be selected. This might require a certain amount of experimentation. 
0x014bf228	a	This attribute allows for specification of how the limiter will behave in a multi-channel case (the norm). If grouped operation is specified, the channel with the lowest current gain will have its gain applied to all channels. This will normally be the channel with the highest signal level, but given attack and release times, and the time constant of the lowpass filter doing the averaging, it could occasionally be a different channel. Use of this mode allows for the preservation of the multi-channel image, since whenever gain is applied, the gain value applied will be the same across all channels. This mode is the default. If independent mode is selected, each channel will use a gain based solely on that channels mean magnitude. This mode might be useful if the user simply wants to compress when it is warrented, on a channel-by-channel basis. 
0x014bf580	a	Channel mode
0x014bf590	a	ATTRIBUTE_SETCHANNELMODE
0x014bf5b0	a	This attribute allows for the specification of the time in seconds over which the limiter turns off, given that the mean magnitude of the input has dropped below the (off) threshold. The sustain of certain limiters can be achieved by a longer release time. 
0x014bf6b4	a	Release time
0x014bf6c4	a	ATTRIBUTE_SETRELEASETIME
0x014bf6e8	a	This attribute specifies the value in dB that the signal mean magnitude must reach before the limiter turns on. An off threshold that is slightly less than this, to achieve some hysteresis, is set automatically to trigger turning the limiter off. Since this limiter threshold must be exceeded by the signal mean magnitude, in order to ensure that signal peaks that exceed the mean are also compressed, a threshold that is much lower than those peaks should be selected. A value greater than or equal to 20.0 dB will turn the limiter off completely, though any value greater than the expected local mean magnitude signal level will also result in no limiting (e.g., if the mean signal level never exceeds -2.0 dB (~0.8), a threshold of 0 or -1 dB will result in the limiter always being off).
0x014bfa00	a	Threshold
0x014bfa0c	a	ATTRIBUTE_SETTHRESHOLD
0x014bfa28	a	ChannelMode specifies how the limiter channel gain should be applied in the multi-channel case.
0x014bfa88	a	ChannelMode
0x014bfa98	a	This value indicates that the limiter should treat the channels as a group, and apply the gain that is smallest across all channels at any given time. 
0x014bfb30	a	Grouped
0x014bfb38	a	CHANNELMODE_GROUPED
0x014bfb50	a	This value indicates that the limiter should treat each channel separately when performing compression. 
0x014bfbbc	a	Independent
0x014bfbc8	a	CHANNELMODE_INDEPENDENT
0x014bfbe3	a	?HighShelfIir2
0x014bfc00	a	The HighShelfIir2 plug-in is a relatively inexpensive filter that can boost or cut the signal spectrum from a selected cutoff frequency up to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate). The filter provides up to +/- 25 dB gain in the gain region, and rolls off at approximately 12 dB per octave to a gain of 0 dB elsewhere. This 0-dB gain characteristic allows for the filter to be chained in series with other filters supporting this characteristic, since it does not attenuate or magnify the signal outside of its gain region. As such, the filter can be useful in a parametric EQ application, where it could provide what is traditionally referred to as treble control. The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear. 
0x014bffb0	a	This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value greater than the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum unmodified, and therefore the filter will be bypassed.  A value of 0 Hertz or less will be clamped to the minimal allowable frequency (very near 0 Hertz) such that the filter remains stable (poles within the unit circle). This results in the specified gain being applied to the entire spectrum.
0x014c0193	a	?HighPassIir2
0x014c01b0	a	The HighPassIir2 plug-in allows for the relatively inexpensive high pass filtering of a signal. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a transition band roll-off of at least 12 dB per octave.The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear; though the degree of non-linearity in the pass band is not severe. Filter stability is assured by clamping the cutoff frequency (internally) to always be BETWEEN zero Hertz and the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate).
0x014c0470	a	This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value of 0 Hertz or less is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value greater than or equal to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) will be clamped to the maximum allowable frequency (very near the Nyquist frequency) such that the filter remains stable (poles within the unit circle). This results in the maximum amount of filtering.
0x014c0647	a	?HighPassFir64
0x014c065f	a	?The HighPassFir64 plug-in is a high quality linear phase highpass filter with excellent isolation. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a minimum of 50 dB stop band attenuation with a very narrow transition bandwidth in between.The filter uses only feed-forward elements, and as such can be classified as a Finite Impulse Response (FIR) filter. The filter is of order 64, utilizing 64 delay elements, and possessing the same number of zeros in its transfer function. Given its FIR nature, the phase response of the filter is linear, and the filter is unconditionally stable.
0x014c08b8	a	This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value of 0 Hertz or less is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value greater than or equal to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate)will be clamped to the Nyquist frequency, resulting in the maximum amount of filtering.
0x014c0a1b	a	?HighPassButterworth
0x014c0a38	a	The HighPassButterworth plug-in provides users with a high pass version of the classic analog IIR Butterworth filter. The Butterworth filter frequency response is referred to as being maximally flat in the passband. What this means in practice is that the pass band frequency response of the filter is very flat, not exhibiting the ripples seen in other filter types, such as the Butterworth's analog cousins, the Chebyshev and Elliptic filters. Also, the frequency response is referred to as being monotonic in the pass and stop bands, which means that as frequency decreases, the amplitude of the response never increases, but rather will either stay the same or decrease. The filter gain in the passband is normalized to 0 dB. Near the filter cutoff frequency (3 dB point), the rolloff of the filter will be around 6 dB per octave per order or pole (see ATTRIBUTE_SETFREQUENCY description). As such, users can select the filter order based on their needs. For example, if the use case requires a filter with maximum rolloff in order to cut out any frequencies below a certain value, the maximum filter order should be chosen. On the other hand if the use case requires a very gentle rolloff in order to model some real world physical acoustic phenomenon, then perhaps selecting a lower order makes the most sense. These filters are guaranteed to be stable, something that is inherent in the digital Butterworth filter design process. 
0x014c0fd8	a	This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. The cutoff frequency is the frequency at which the amplitude response of the filter is 3 dB below its value in the passband (nominally 0 dB). This is also sometimes referred to as the knee of the frequency response. A value of 0 Hz is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value of the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) results in the maximum amount of filtering. Note that in order to avoid excessively long filter coefficient calculation times on some platforms (PC), ATTRIBUTE_SETFREQUENCY is clamped to stay within 1% of the Nyquist frequency at both DC at the low end of the spectrum, and the Nyquist frequency at the high end. Given an output sample rate of 48000 Hz for example, this would result in a minimum cutoff frequency of 240 Hz (1% of 48000/2), and a maximum (before the filter turns off) of 23670 Hz (48000/2 - (1% of 48000/2)). 
0x014c13a7	a	?GainFader
0x014c13c8	a	The GainFader plug-in provides a mechanism for changing the audio signal gain at a specified start time, over some time period, and according to some amplitude change characteristic. A start time for the change is specified in seconds. This requires the plug-in to determine the current system time (via the GetTime() system call), and specify the start time based on that. For example, if the desired start time is 1 second from the current time, 1 would be added to the current time and specified as the start time. A start time of 0.0 will always cause the fade to start immediately. A fade time is also specified in seconds, and denotes the duration of the amplitude change. This change (or fade) will then occur over the fade time duration to smoothly arrive at the specified final gain value, given by the endGain parameter. If a fade is already in progress when a new fade request is made, the new fade request will be applied starting at the most recent gain value of the fade that was in progress. Even if this new fade request has a start time in the future, any fade that is currently in progress will be stopped at the most recent gain value. If the specified start time of a fade is already in the past, but the projected end time of the fade has not yet passed, the fade is started immediately. If the projected end time is also in the past, the fade is cancelled. Currently three fade characteristics are supported; linear amplitude, linear power, and sine amplitude.
0x014c1994	a	Note also that it is possible to explicitly set a start gain prior to a given fade. This can be achieved by specifying a fade of duration 0 with a start time of 0 and an end gain equal to the gain value desired. This will only take if both these parameters (fadeTime and startTime) are set to 0. One example of when this feature might be useful is if the user wants to ramp up a signal from 0 but the current gain of the fader is not 0, such as at plug-in startup (when the gain is set to 1). In this case, the user would specify two fades in succession, one to set the current gain to 0, and a second one to initiate the ramping up of the signal. Since the user may require that the initial gain setting (to 0 in this case) not delay the subsequent signal ramp up, both these gain fade events may be applied within the same lock (rw::audio::core::Lock()/Unlock()). As such, it is a special case, since otherwise the Gain Fader does not support queuing of gain fade events. It should also be noted that this feature should be used with extreme caution, since changing the gain value over a fade duration of 0 will introduce a click if the plug-in is passing a signal at the time the gain is changed. It should only be used if there is no signal being passed or it is known that the current signal amplitude is 0.
0x014c1eb8	a	This event results in the scheduling of a gain fade.

0x014c257f	a	?Delay
0x014c25a0	a	The Delay plug-in allows for the application of delay to the audio signal. This delay can be applied with a variable amount of feedback, ranging from no feedback (0) to near full feedback (0.99). The delay amount is specified in seconds, and a delay of 0 turns the plug-in off. The plug-in allows for dynamically changing the delay time and feedback amount by smoothly cross fading from one set of parameters to another. This cross fade currently occurs over a 128 sample period. In the case where a larger buffer is required due to an increased delay time, the plug-in will allocate a larger buffer and seamlessly transfer the existing buffered data to it. It will not start the cross fade until this new larger buffer has filled up the new sample locations, which is the time difference between the new delay time and that supported by the previous buffer size. The buffer will not be resized when a smaller delay time is requested, though this does not effect the delay itself, only how much buffer it is using. Use of the delay with a moderate amount of feedback and appropriate delay will produce an echo effect. Use of the delay without feedback might be useful to adjust the relative timing between different voices in the mix, or to produce a single echo relative to the dry signal path.
0x014c2ab0	a	This attribute specifies the feedback coefficient of the plug-in. It can range from -0.99 through to 0.99. While there is some difference in effect between a positive and negative gain of the same magnitude (positive has a pole at DC, and therefore a larger DC gain), in practice it is unlikely that there will be much of a discernable difference.
0x014c2c0c	a	Feedback
0x014c2c18	a	ATTRIBUTE_SETFEEDBACK
0x014c2c30	a	This attribute specifies the delay amount in seconds. The delay is in the feedforward path of the plug-in, and as such all audio passing through the plug-in will be delayed by this amount.
0x014c2cf0	a	Delay Time
0x014c2cfc	a	ATTRIBUTE_SETDELAYTIME
0x014c2d18	a	This attribute specifies the anticipated maximum delay amount in seconds. The value can be set by the user to force the plugin to allocate sufficient memory during initialisation. 0x014c2e41	a	If the user does not specify this amount, or if ATTRIBUTE_SETDELAYTIME exceeds this amount at any time, then the plugin will re-allocate its buffers when it needs to, which users may wish to avoid for efficiency.

0x014c2f2b	a	?Dac

0x014c2fc7	a	qRWAudioCore Dac
0x014c2fd8	a	The Dac plugin is responsible for taking the RWAudioCore mixer output and ultimately getting it out the speakers so the user can hear the output of RWAudioCore.
0x014c307a	a	The Dac plugin is special in that it is typically the only true output plugin. This means it actually drives the RWAudioCore mixing process. The Dac plugin will hook itself into some kind of periodic update from a self created thread, or hook into a callback from a lower level audio system. It will then periodically determine how many samples need to be mixed and then call into the RWAudioCore mixer. It will then format the RWAudioCore mixer output appropriately and send it off to the lower level audio system.
0x014c327f	a	The Dac plugin should typically only ever be inserted on the mastering voice. It should always be inserted as the last plugin on the voice. There should also be only one Dac plugin active in the entire RWAudioCore system. The management of the Dac plugin should typically be done by game code, not by data driven audio tools.
0x014c33c6	a	On the PC, the Dac sends its output through a DirectSound secondary buffer with the cooperative level set to PRIORITY, and the focus set to GLOBAL. The priority used allows other code to create DirectSound buffers outside of RWAudioCore if required, and the code should get along (they will both be heard). The GLOBAL focus means that even if the application loses focus, RWAudioCore will still be heard (this greatly simplifies the complexities of handling focus at a low level). If the application does not wish to continue being heard when losing focus, it should set a master volume gain plugin on the mastering voice to 0.0 when focus is lost, and set it back to its previous value when focus is regained. If the application wishes to completely pause audio when it loses focus, it can alternatively call the rw::audio::core::Dac::STOP and rw::audio::core::Dac::START events.
0x014c3738	a	On the Xbox 360, the Dac sends its output through an XAudio voice. Game code is free to use other XAudio voices, meaning both RWAudioCore and other audio systems can output to the speakers at the same time.
0x014c3808	a	On the PS3, the Dac outputs audio via Sony's libaudio library. One of the libaudio ports is used, leaving three others free for other audio middleware to coexist peacefully with RWAC. As a convenience, the Dac upon creation will initialize the PS3 audio system with cellAudioInit() if it has not already been initialized by other code game code or audio middleware. In most cases it is likely RWAC will be the only low level audio middleware being used so game code will not have to deal with the PS3 audio system at all. If cellAudioInit() is initialized by the Dac then it will also call cellAudioQuit() when it is released, otherwise it will not be called. This means if cellAudioInit() has already been called before the Dac is created, the Dac should be released before cellAudioQuit() is called to ensure proper matching calls to cellAudioInit() and cellAudioQuit().
0x014c3b72	a	On the Wii, the Dac sends its software mixed output through AX AuxB callback when the Dac is started.  The Dac upon creation will initialize the Wii AI and AX libraries by calling AIInit() and AXInit().  This means that the Dac will also be shutting down AI and AX by calling AIReset() and AXQuit() during Dac release to ensure proper matching calls.  Games that use AI and AX outside of RWAC should beware of these calls made in the Dac release (i.e. when releasing the voice with the Dac plugin or when releasing the RWAC System.  Note that stopping the Dac does not shutdown the AI and AX).  In the case of the Wii Home Menu, the game code should pause the master voice before entering the Wii Home Menu.  
0x014c3e38	a	The offline-mix-frame event can only be issued when the Dac has been started in offline mode.  The offline-mix-frame event processes immediately, and causes the Dac to execute all commands in the command queue and produce a single mix frame.  Note that when the Dac is in offline mode, the mix frames that are produced are not sent to the audio hardware.
0x014c3f9c	a	Offline mix frame
0x014c3fb0	a	EVENT_OFFLINEMIXFRAME
0x014c3fc8	a	This event will stop the Dac from driving the RWAudioCore mixer and sending output to the speakers.
0x014c4030	a	The start2 event tells the Dac plugin to begin operation.  When started in realtime mode, the Dac will begin driving the RWAudioCore mixer process and outputting the resulting sample data to the speakers.  When started in offline mode, the Dac will enter a state where one mix frame is processed for each EVENT_OFFLINEMIXFRAME event.  In offline mode, sample data is not sent to the speakers.  If you wish to use the audio data that is produced you will need to use the SampleCapture or AiffWriter plugins, or a suitable replacement.
0x014c4247	a	The start2 event must be called in order for any RWAudioCore playback to occur. The Dac is not started upon instantiation.
0x014c42c3	a	On the PC platform, it is possible that no sound card is installed on a machine or the sound system is not working for some reason. When this happens, the Dac provides functionality to "pretend" that it is operating normally as if a sound card is present. RWAudioCore will act as if nothing is wrong. This functionality is provided so that game programmers can handle the missing sound system with ease. The alternative is that special code must be inserted all over the place to handle RWAudioCore API functions not working as expected.
0x014c44e0	a	Start2
0x014c44e8	a	Start2Params
0x014c44f8	a	EVENT_START2
0x014c4508	a	This method is deprecated.  Please use the Start2 method instead.
0x014c4550	a	The set sample rate event allows a game to set the Dac output sample rate to a new sample rate.
0x014c45b1	a	The Dac output sample rate affects the overall audio fidelity of RWAudioCore output. Higher sample rates mean higher quality, but more CPU usage.
0x014c4644	a	The Dac supports a standard set of sample rates; 22050, 24000, 32000, 44100, 48000, 96000, and 192000. The actual sample rates supported on each platform will be a subset of these. Xbox 360 currently only supports 48000 and Wii currently only supports 32000 for example.
0x014c4754	a	The sample rate should ideally be set before the Dac is started. However it can be changed while the Dac is running with minimal side effects to the RWAudioCore code or game code. The audio output will be interrupted for a brief period, which can sound disconcerting. If this is an issue the signal on the mastering voice could be faded down and back around the switch by manipulating a fader plugin appropriately. Note that pitch sensitive plugins must adjust to the new output sample rate on the fly. Most pitch sensitive plugins will handle this automatically, but some pitch sensitive plugins will choose not to handle this automatically as doing so could lead to large inefficiencies. For example, a convolution reverb requires an impulse response recorded at the same sample rate as the output sample rate. Switching the output sample rate dynamically causes the reverb engine to not be using the correct impulse response. In that case the reverb plugin must be stopped before the sample rate switch, and then started again with the correct impulse response after the sample rate switch.
0x014c4b9b	a	Note there is no way to ask what sample rates are supported by the Dac. The reality is it is fairly pointless to know this information as there isn't anything that can really be done about it programmatically. When this event is called to set a sample rate, the Dac will latch to the sample rate specified, or choose the next highest sample rate supported if the rate specified was not supported. If no higher rate is supported, then the next lowest rate is chosen. The game code should simply choose the desired sample rate and then let the Dac choose the most appropriate sample rate based on the specified rate. The sample rates supported and the rate chosen are printed to the console for the debug version of RWAudioCore if this information is of interest.
0x014c4e98	a	Set sample rate
0x014c4ea8	a	SetSampleRateParams
0x014c4ebc	a	EVENT_SETSAMPLERATE
0x014c4ed0	a	The set mode event allows a game to set the Dac output to a new output mode.
0x014c4f1e	a	If the specified output mode is not supported on the given platform/configuration, the next most logical output mode will be notched down to and selected automatically. This means the output mode could always be set to 5.1 if desired and the closest supported mode will be selected.
0x014c503a	a	The output mode should ideally be set before the Dac is started. However it can be changed while the Dac is running without any side effects to the RWAudioCore code or game code. The audio output will be interrupted for a brief period, which can sound disconcerting. If this is an issue the signal on the mastering voice could be faded down and back around the switch by manipulating a fader plugin appropriately.
0x014c51d9	a	The Xbox 360 ignores the SETMODE event. All output mode selection is handled in the Xbox 360 dashboard screen. RWAC always feeds a 5.1 signal to the Xbox 360 and it will down-mix or encode the audio into the format the user has selected in the dashboard.
0x014c52d9	a	Unlike the Xbox 360, the PS3 does not automatically encode audio into the format the user has selected in the OSD.RWAC looks at what audio output modes are set up in the OSD and selects the one it feels will yield the highest quality results. The selection criteria first attempts 8 channel modes, 6 channel modes, then 2 channel modes. Within each channel selection it will attempt the encoding method that introduces the least distortion into the signal. Given only some of the OSD modes are supported this effectively results in the order of selection being HDMI 7.1, HDMI 5.1, HDMI or optical Dolby Digital 5.1, then HDMI or optical or analog 2 channel. Note at this time there is a known issue with HDMI 7.1 output so even if this is the only mode selected in the OSD you will actually get HDMI 5.1 as 5.1 works.
0x014c560c	a	On the PS3 the SETMODE event is respected in a specific situation in regards to desiring Dolby Pro Logic II output or plain stereo. The OSD and Sony libraries lack native support for PL2. Some game teams have requested PL2 support on PS3 so RWAC provides it via its own encoder. Unfortunately there is no way to select PL2 in the OSD as it doesn't support it. Switching between stereo and PL2 must be managed by calling SETMODE with either MODE_STEREO or MODE_PROLOGIC2. In this situation the SETMODE event acts more like a hint than an absolute setting. MODE_STEREO or MODE_PROLOGIC2 will only be respected if the OSD is set to output only 2 channels. Setting of other modes will be ignored, e.g. setting MODE_5POINT1 is meaningless as RWAC only respects the OSD when determining 5.1 output.
0x014c5926	a	A PS3 game that wishes to support PL2 is faced with one of two choices on how to know when to enable it. The game code could have a front end menu option to allow the user to select between stereo and PL2. This is however very ugly to the end user as they now have to configure audio output in more than one place. The option is also confusing as it doesn't force stereo or PL2 mode if the OSD is set to a 5.1 or 7.1 output mode. For these reasons a menu option is not recommended. The saner alternative is to decide up front if your game will always output either plain stereo or PL2. This would probably be the audio director's choice based on what they prefer.
0x014c5bbf	a	On the Wii platform RWAC supports Pro Logic II and stereo, where RWAC defaults to PL2 mode.  PL2 encoding is provided by the Wii's Pro Logic II encoder.  Note that RWAC sets the Wii AX mode according to the output mode setting, so beware of this when the AX library is used outside of RWAC.
0x014c5ce3	a	On the PC platform, note that some output modes are not reliable, even if they are enumerated as supported (e.g. quad and 5.1). Although everything may appear to work correctly when interfacing with RWAudioCore, the user may experience crackling audio or missing channels for example. For that matter the quad and 5.1 modes will almost always be enumerated as supported even if the user only has two speakers. As the quad and 5.1 modes cannot be counted on to work on every machine, the game must provide an option to let the user switch between stereo, quad, and 5.1 modes. It is also a bonus to the user if an audio testing screen is included where a sound is played in turn on all channels. This way the user can determine if the quad or 5.1 modes will work properly for them in the game.
0x014c5ffc	a	When a mode other than 5.1 is set, the Dac plugin will perform an industry standard fold-down to the quad, stereo or mono output mode. Even if the Dac is not performing the fold-down, the PC or console platform might be performing a fold-down on its end. Hard clipping can be introduced during the fold-down if the signal coming into the fold-down is loud. Although the hard clipping should not be as audible a problem as it might sound, it can be avoided by reducing the volume of the 5.1 signal before it goes into the Dac plugin. If a signal is normalized to full scale (+/- 1.0) before it enters the Dac plugin by a plugin such as a compressor (strongly suggested), then when folding down to quad reducing the signal to 58% (-4.7 dB) of it's original level will guarantee no hard clipping will occur. When folding down to stereo, 37% (-8.6 dB) will guarantee no clipping. In reality, hard clipping noise is not as noticeable as one might think, and it may also be difficult drive the 5.1 signal to produce the extreme configuration required that will give a maximum peak after fold-down. For this reason it is never suggested that the volume be reduced to entirely reduce clipping (because a lot of volume will be lost), but instead find a happy medium through common sense and experimentation (e.g. somewhere between 0 and -6 dB).

0x014c6b20	a	This attribute indicates how much latency in time there is between making an RWAudioCore call, and when the results of the action are heard out of the speakers (e.g. from the time a sample is triggered with an RWAudioCore call and the time it begins being heard out of the speakers). The units are in seconds.
0x014c6c57	a	In an ideal world, there would be no latency between triggering an action and hearing it out of the speakers. On platforms such as the PC considerable latency (e.g. up to 300 milliseconds) can be introduced because the PC is not a real-time system. The latency will vary on the PC as there is dynamic compensation based on how "real-time" the PC is acting at the moment. On other platforms latency is not much of an issue.
0x014c6dff	a	Latency becomes a large factor when trying to synchronize other game concepts such as animation or video to audio. If the latency is not taken into account and the Dac is adding 300 milliseconds of latency, then the video will be out of sync with the audio by 300 milliseconds. This will make for an unpleasant end user experience. Game code and other systems should get around this by compensating for the latency introduced. If the latency was 300 milliseconds, then 300 milliseconds should be subtracted from the current playback position of a sample being played which is being synchronized. The current latency must be subtracted from the sample playback position each time it is polled, otherwise drift could occur as latency changes. This will keep the audio and video in sync.

0x014c7630	a	StartMode
0x014c7640	a	Offline mode is an alternate mode of operation for the Dac.  This mode is useful for situations in which the Dac needs to be run in non-realtime (i.e. faster or slower than realtime).  Examples of when offline mode is useful include both offline testing (where tests can be run faster than realtime in order to save time) and in-game video encoding (where the video compression process may be slower than realtime, requiring the audio playback to be slowed down accordingly).  When in offline mode, audio commands are executed and mix frames are produced by issuing the EVENT_OFFLINEMIXFRAME event.  The audio clock does not advance unless mix frames are produced, so offline mode and EVENT_OFFLINEMIXFRAME can be used to control the speed at which audio is processed and sent to the hardware.

0x014c7eac	a	It should be noted PL2 is not a discreet surround format, surround information is derived from phase and amplitude information encoded into the signals. Surround in PL2 will not likely sound the exact same as discreet 5.1, in particular phantom center sounds will collapse to the center. The signal encoding process may also introduce some audio issues when played back on stereo equipment.
0x014c8034	a	On Wii, this is the default output mode. The entire mix is then brought down an additional 3 dB to keep the signal from running too hot and clipping excessively where it wouldn't have in the 5.1 mix.The down mixed signals are then sent to the Wii Dolby Pro Logic II encoder for PL2 encoding through AuxB callback.

0x014c8a87	a	?Compressor1
0x014c8a9f	a	?The Compressor1 plug-in provides a means for dynamic range reduction of the signal. When the mean magnitude of the signal exceeds some settable threshold, a gain term is applied to the signal to reduce the overall amplitude. This gain will only cut in fully after a specified attack time, and conversely, will only be fully released when the mean magnitude is below the threshold (including hysteresis) for a specified release time. The gain amount is based on a settable ratio, and can be derived on a per channel basis, or can be based on the maximum mean magnitude channel across all channels. The punchiness characteristic of some compressors can be achieved by setting a relatively longer attack time ( > 1 second), while the sustain of certain compressors can be achieved by a longer release time. It should be noted that since the compressor threshold must be exceeded by the signal mean magnitude (via a lowpass filter with very low cutoff), in order to ensure that signal peaks that far exceed the mean are also compressed, a threshold that is much lower than those peaks must be selected. This might require a certain amount of experimentation. 
0x014c8f28	a	This attribute allows for specification of how the compressor will behave in a multi-channel case (the norm). If grouped operation is specified, the channel with the lowest current gain will have its gain applied to all channels. This will normally be the channel with the highest signal level, but given attack and release times, and the time constant of the lowpass filter doing the averaging, it could occasionally be a different channel. Use of this mode allows for the preservation of the multi-channel image, since whenever gain is applied, the gain value applied will be the same across all channels. This mode is the default. If independent mode is selected, each channel will use a gain based solely on that channels mean magnitude. This mode might be useful if the user simply wants to compress when it is warrented, on a channel-by-channel basis. 
0x014c9288	a	This attribute allows for the specification of the time in seconds over which the compressor turns off, given that the mean magnitude of the input has dropped below the (off) threshold. The sustain of certain compressors can be achieved by a longer release time. 
0x014c9390	a	This attribute allows for the specification of the time in seconds over which the compressor reaches full compression, given that the mean magnitude of the input has exceeded the threshold. The punchiness characteristic of some compressors can be achieved by setting a relatively longer attack time ( > 1 second).
0x014c94cc	a	Attack time
0x014c94d8	a	ATTRIBUTE_SETATTACKTIME
0x014c94f0	a	This attribute specifies the dB ratio between output and input signal levels when the compressor is in the on state. For example, a ratio of 5 means that, a 5 dB increase in the input signal level will result in a 1 dB increase in the output signal level. 
0x014c95f4	a	Ratio
0x014c95fc	a	ATTRIBUTE_SETRATIO
0x014c9610	a	This attribute specifies the value in dB that the signal mean magnitude must reach before the compressor turns on. An off threshold that is slightly less than this, to achieve some hysteresis, is set automatically to trigger turning the compressor off. Since this compressor threshold must be exceeded by the signal mean magnitude, in order to ensure that signal peaks that exceed the mean are also compressed, a threshold that is much lower than those peaks should be selected. A value greater than or equal to 20.0 dB will turn the compressor off completely, though any value greater than the expected local mean magnitude signal level will also result in no compression (e.g., if the mean signal level never exceeds -2.0 dB (~0.8), a threshold of 0 or -1 dB will result in the compressor always being off).
0x014c9940	a	ChannelMode specifies how the compressor channel gain should be applied in the multi-channel case.
0x014c99a8	a	This value indicates that the compressor should treat the channels as a group, and apply the gain that is smallest across all channels at any given time. 
0x014c9a48	a	This value indicates that the compressor should treat each channel separately when performing compression. 
0x014c9ab7	a	?BandPassIir2
0x014c9acf	a	?The BandPassIir2 plug-in allows for the relatively inexpensive band pass filtering of a signal. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a transition band roll-off of at least 12 dB per octave.The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear; though the degree of non-linearity in the pass band is not severe. Filter stability is assured by clamping the cutoff frequency (internally) to always be BETWEEN zero Hertz and the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate), and the bandwidth to be greater than zero. 
0x014c9dc0	a	This attribute specifies the filter bandwidth in Hertz. A value greater than or equal to twice the Nyquist frequency (which equals the sample rate) will always result in passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. Lesser bandwidths will also turn the filter off, if the center frequency is appropriately set. For example, if the center frequency is in the middle of the band, a bandwidth greater than or equal to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) would be wide enough to take in the entire band, and therefore turn off the filter. A bandwidth of zero will result in the maximum amount of filtering, and will be clamped internally to something slightly larger than zero to ensure filter stability (Filter Q = frequency/bandwdith is currently limited to a maximum of 20). 
0x014ca0f0	a	Bandwidth
0x014ca0fc	a	ATTRIBUTE_SETBANDWIDTH
0x014ca114	a	This attribute specifies the filter center frequency in Hertz.
0x014ca15b	a	?BandPassFir64
0x014ca178	a	The BandPassFir64 plug-in is a high quality linear phase bandpass filter with excellent isolation. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a minimum of 50 dB stop band attenuation with a very narrow transition bandwidth in between.The filter uses only feed-forward elements, and as such can be classified as a Finite Impulse Response (FIR) filter. The filter is of order 64, utilizing 64 delay elements, and possessing the same number of zeros in its transfer function. Given its FIR nature, the phase response of the filter is linear, and the filter is unconditionally stable.
0x014ca3d0	a	This attribute specifies the filter bandwidth in Hertz. A value greater than or equal to twice the Nyquist frequency (which equals the sample rate) will always result in passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. Lesser bandwidths will also turn the filter off, if the center frequency is appropriately set. For example, if the center frequency is in the middle of the band, a bandwidth greater than or equal to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) would be wide enough to take in the entire band, and therefore turn off the filter. A bandwidth of zero will result in the maximum amount of filtering.
